[n: Joan. vi, 38.] For he who wants to do this and that, the things for which he begs off, either shows himself more discerning than the one who commands him, or is mocked by demons; both of which are evil and devilish. ⟦q27.13|1+2+3+4⟧ So thou oughtest to obey in everything. ⟦q27.13|1+2+3+4⟧ For thy abba who commands thee bears thy judgment, as one who will be required to give account for thee. ⟦q27.13|5⟧ If what is commanded seems heavy to thee, ask him, and leave the matter to his discernment. ⟦q27.13|6⟧ But if it is brethren [bid:c1817d]who have given the command, and thou seest or thinkest that the matter has harm in it, or is beyond thy strength, again ask the abba, and what he tells thee, do; for if thou wilt judge not only the matters but also the men, thou wilt draw affliction on thyself. ⟦q27.13|7⟧ But where the matter seems to be good, there show obedience to the brethren; and where the thought hesitates, or it is beyond thy strength, or has harm, lay it before thy abba, and what he discerns, do. ⟦q27.13|8⟧ For he knows what he is doing, and how he cares for thy [1820] [bid:c1820a]soul; and be at rest. ⟦q27.13|9⟧ Believe then that whatever he says to thee is according to God; and everything according to God is profitable, and brings no grief or agitation. ⟦q27.13|10+11+12+13⟧ For from good no evil comes forth: "For every good tree brings forth good fruits." [n: Matth. vii. 17.] And as for someone's asking thy help with the abba, do it as fulfilling a commandment which thou hast heard from the abba to do. ⟦q27.13|10+11+12+13⟧ For if he sets thee as door-keeper, saying, "Tell me of every man who comes," dost thou count thyself something of thyself, or fulfil an order? ⟦q27.13|10+11+12+13⟧ If the abba says to tell him, or not to tell, thou hast nothing to do with it. ⟦q27.13|14⟧ And as for when thou goest into a brother's cell, or he himself tells thee to pray: up to the third time say, "Forgive," and if after the third he persists, then do it with lowliness; and do thou too say it to someone three times, and if he does not want [bid:c1820b]to, stop in peace, and do not afflict him. ⟦q27.13|15⟧ And this is the true way of God, in which take heed to thyself to work without hypocrisy, in a pure heart, God giving thee his hand and helping thee by his grace.

⟦q27.13|15⟧ Question 7.

⟦q27.14|1+2⟧ Ought one to condescend to the body in the matter of diet when one has not yet entirely broken down? ⟦q27.14|3⟧ And if someone is in poor habit with regard to the usual food; and if someone, suffering nothing, declines this food as producing this passion, does he do well?

⟦q27.15|1⟧ Answer.

⟦q27.15|2⟧ If a man sees himself not troubled by [bid:c1820c]passion, nor warred on, he ought to condescend a little to the body; but if he is warred on, no. ⟦q27.15|3⟧ For it is plain that this apparent weakness too is from the demons. ⟦q27.15|4⟧ For in true weakness, the kind that is from God, the warfare too slackens. ⟦q27.15|5⟧ So, where there is warfare, it is profitable to wear the body down, even if it falls into sickness; for it brings the soul out. ⟦q27.15|6⟧ But if the cause of the irregularity is plain, and if perhaps from a journey, or from severe wounds, the body is sick, one ought to condescend, but with gravity and not beyond what is needed; since the demons too are busy at their own. ⟦q27.15|7+8⟧ As for poor habit, there are times when one can compel the thought a little; but if one cannot, one condescends a little. ⟦q27.15|9⟧ For this too is a weakness. ⟦q27.15|10⟧ But if anyone [bid:c1820d]learns that this food produces passion, he ought not to eat of it; for it is found to be a defeat for him, and he ought to be on his guard. ⟦q27.15|11⟧ Strive not to cast this into forgetfulness, and believe that with those who are helped and saved by God thou art being saved.

⟦q27.15|11⟧ Question 8.

⟦q27.16|1+2⟧ If, eating with the brethren, I have taken what I need sooner, is it good that I rise, or should I stay until all rise? ⟦q27.16|3⟧ And if I measure out the portion of my bread over all the relishes, do I do better?

⟦q27.17|1+2⟧ Answer.

⟦q27.17|1+2⟧ Sitting at table, if thou seest that thy need [1821] [bid:c1821a]is met, and thou canst not sit without eating, rise. But to sit and not eat is greater than that. And to make thy morsel stretch [sic: *ἀπεκτεῖναι*] over all the cooked dishes is better than both.

⟦q27.17|1+2⟧ Question 9.

⟦q27.18|1+2⟧ What shall I do, since I fear the shame of dishonour; and if I fall into conversation with certain people, I am drawn into it greatly and taken captive, so as almost to forget myself; and always too I am, as it were, ashamed to leave them?

⟦q27.19|1⟧ Answer.

⟦q27.19|2⟧ If it tends to edification, and there is no cutting off of a more necessary matter, stay while it is being [bid:c1821b]discussed; but if it is unprofitable, say, "Forgive; I am still weak." ⟦q27.19|3⟧ And as for shame, consider the shame before all the people which will be in the Lord's presence for sinners, and count the temporary one as nothing.

⟦q28.1|1⟧ Instruction XXII. ⟦q28.2|1⟧ *Of the same holy Dorotheus: an interpretation of some sayings of St Gregory which are sung with troparia at holy Pascha.*

⟦q28.3|1⟧ 1. ⟦q28.3|2⟧ I would gladly speak to you a little about the psalms we sing, so that you may not drift off after the tune, [sic: *ἀπομετεωρίζεσθαι*] but so that your mind itself may also be set on fire, in its measure, by the power of the words. ⟦q28.3|3⟧ What did we sing just now? ⟦q28.3|4+5⟧ *Day of resurrection: let us offer ourselves as fruit.* [bid:c1821c]Since in old times the sons of Israel, at feasts or at festal gatherings, brought gifts to the Lord according to the law, such as sacrifices, whole burnt offerings, first-fruits and all such things, St Gregory urges you to keep the feast to the Lord as they did, and he exhorts you, saying: *Day of resurrection*; that is, instead of saying, the day of the holy feast, the day of the divine festival, the day of the Pascha of Christ. ⟦q28.3|6⟧ What is the Pascha of Christ? ⟦q28.3|7⟧ The sons of Israel kept the Phasekh when they came out of Egypt; but the Pascha which St Gregory now urges us to keep is kept by the soul which comes out of the spiritual Egypt, that is, out of sin. ⟦q28.3|8+9⟧ For when the soul passes over from sin to virtue, then it keeps the Phasekh to the Lord, as Evagrius said: *The Pascha of the Lord is a passing over from evil.* ⟦q28.3|10⟧ So today is [bid:c1821d]the Pascha of the Lord, the day of a bright feast, the day of the resurrection of Christ, who crucified sin, and died for us and rose again. ⟦q28.3|11⟧ So let us too bring gifts to the Lord, sacrifices, whole burnt offerings, not of irrational animals, which Christ does not want; for sacrifice and offering of irrational beasts he did not wish, and whole burnt offerings of calves and sheep he took no pleasure in. ⟦q28.3|12⟧ And Isaiah says: *What to me is the multitude of your sacrifices? says the Lord*, [n: Isa. i, 11.] and so on. ⟦q28.3|13⟧ But since the Lamb of God was sacrificed for us, according to the Apostle, who says: *For indeed our Pascha has been sacrificed,* [1824] [bid:c1824a]*Christ*, [n: I Cor. v, 7.] that he might take away the sin of the world; and: *He became a curse for us*, as it is written: *Cursed is everyone who hangs on a tree*, [n: Galat. iii, 13.] that he might redeem us from the curse of the law; and: that we might receive the adoption of sons: [n: Galat. iv, 5.] then we too ought to bring him something pleasing as our gift.

⟦q28.4|1+2⟧ 2. [sic: *Δʹ*] What gift, then, or what sacrifice ought we to bring to Christ on the day of the resurrection, so as to please him, since he does not want sacrifices of irrational animals? ⟦q28.4|3+4+5+6+7+8+9+10+11⟧ The saint himself teaches us this too; for having said, *Day of resurrection*, he added, [bid:c1824b]*let us offer ourselves as fruit*; as the Apostle too says: *Present your bodies a living sacrifice, holy, well-pleasing to the Lord, your reasonable worship.* [n: Rom. xii, 1.] How then ought we to present our bodies a living sacrifice, holy to God? ⟦q28.4|3+4+5+6+7+8+9+10+11⟧ By no longer doing the wills of the flesh and of our minds, but walking by the Spirit and not fulfilling the desire of the flesh. [n: Galat. v, 16.] For this is to put to death the members that are on the earth. [n: Coloss. iii, 5.] This is called a living sacrifice; since the irrational animal that is led away for sacrifice is sacrificed at once [sic: *ἐνιαυτῷ*] and dies. ⟦q28.4|3+4+5+6+7+8+9+10+11⟧ The saints, offering themselves as fruit to God, sacrifice themselves to God while living, every day, as David says: *For thy sake we are put to death all the day long; we were [bid:c1824c]counted as sheep for slaughter.* [n: Psal. XLIII, 22; Rom. viii, 36.] This is what St Gregory says: *Let us offer ourselves as fruit*; that is, let us sacrifice ourselves, let us put ourselves to death all the day long, as all the saints too did, for the sake of Christ our God, for the sake of him who died for us. ⟦q28.4|3+4+5+6+7+8+9+10+11⟧ And how did they put themselves to death? ⟦q28.4|3+4+5+6+7+8+9+10+11⟧ By not loving the world nor the things in the world, as it says in the Catholic Epistles, but by renouncing the desire of the flesh and the desire of the eyes and the arrogance of life, [n: I Joan. ii, 15.] that is, love of pleasure, love of money, vainglory; and by taking up the cross and following Christ, and crucifying the world to themselves, and themselves to the world. ⟦q28.4|12+13⟧ Of these the Apostle says: *And those who are Christ Jesus' have crucified the flesh with its passions and desires.* [n: Galat. v, 24.] See, this is how the saints put themselves to death.

⟦q28.5|1+2⟧ 3. [bid:c1824d]And how did they offer themselves as fruit? ⟦q28.5|3+4⟧ By not living for themselves, but making themselves slaves to the commandments of God, and leaving their own wills for the commandment and the love of God and of neighbour; as St Peter said: *See, we have left everything and followed thee.* [n: Matth. xix, 27] What did he leave? ⟦q28.5|3+4⟧ He had no money, did he, or possessions, or gold, or silver? ⟦q28.5|5+6⟧ He had a net, and an old one at that, as St John Chrysostom said; and he left, as he said, all his own wills, every attachment to this age; so that it is plain that if he had had money [1825] [bid:c1825a]or property, he would have despised that too; and taking up the cross he followed Christ, according to the saying: *I live, yet no longer I, but Christ lives in me.* [n: Galat. ii, 20.] See, this is how the saints offered themselves as fruit, putting themselves to death, as we said, to every attachment and to their own will, and living for Christ alone and for his commandments. ⟦q28.5|7⟧ So let us too offer ourselves as fruit, as St Gregory teaches; for he wants us, God's most precious possession. ⟦q28.5|8⟧ Truly, man is more precious than all the visible creatures. ⟦q28.5|9⟧ For those the Creator brought forth by a word, saying: *Let this be*, and it was; and again: *Let the earth bring forth this*, and it was; and: *Let the waters bring forth*, [n: Gen. i, 3.] and all such things. ⟦q28.5|10⟧ But man he formed and adorned with his own hands, and [bid:c1825b]all those things he appointed for the service and rest of man, and man himself he set up as king over all of them, and made him enjoy the delight of paradise. ⟦q28.5|11⟧ And what is more wonderful: when he had fallen from there through his own sin, he called him back again through the blood of his only-begotten Son; so that of all visible things man is God's most precious possession; and not only most precious, as the saint said, but also most his own. ⟦q28.5|11⟧ For: *Let us make man according to our image and according to likeness.* ⟦q28.5|12+13⟧ And again: *God made man; in the image of God he made him; and he breathed into his face the breath of life.* [n: ibid. 26.] And our Lord himself, coming to dwell among us, took [bid:c1825c]the form of a man, the flesh of a man, of a new man; and, to put it simply, he becomes everything that man is, except sin.

⟦q28.6|1⟧ 4. ⟦q28.6|2⟧ And so we have made man closely our own, so to speak, appropriated him [cj: read προσῳκειώσατο … ἰδιοποιήσατο, "he made man closely his own … appropriated him"]. ⟦q28.6|3⟧ Well and fittingly then the saint said: *Man is the most precious possession*; then he adds still more plainly: *Let us render to the image what is according to the image.* ⟦q28.6|3⟧ How is this too? ⟦q28.6|4⟧ Let us learn from the Apostle, who says: *Let us cleanse ourselves from every defilement of flesh and spirit.* [n: II Cor. vii, 1.] Let us make our image pure, as we received it; let us wipe it clean [sic: *ἀποσμύξωμεν*] of the filth of sin, so that its beauty may show through the virtues. ⟦q28.6|5+6⟧ For [bid:c1825d]this beauty David prays, saying: *Lord, in thy will grant power to my beauty.* [n: Psal. xxix, 8.] So let us cleanse what in us is according to the image; for God wants it [sic: *αὐτῷ*] from us, and he gave it having no spot or wrinkle or anything of the kind. ⟦q28.6|7+8⟧ Let us render to the image what is according to the image. [sic: *τῷ κατ᾿ εἰκόνα*] Let us know our dignity; let us learn of what great goods we have been counted worthy; let us learn in whose image we have been made; let us not be ignorant of the great things given us by him, through his goodness alone, not through our own worth; [1828] [bid:c1828a]let us know that we have been made according to the image of God who made us. ⟦q28.6|9⟧ Let us honour the archetype. ⟦q28.6|10⟧ Let us not insult the image of God according to which we were made. ⟦q28.6|11⟧ Who, wanting to paint an image of the emperor, dares to put a rotten colour in the image, and to dishonour the emperor and be punished? ⟦q28.6|12⟧ Rather, he puts in all colours that are precious and bright and worthy of the emperor's image. ⟦q28.6|13⟧ And sometimes gold leaf too is set into the images of emperors, and the painter takes pains to set into the image, as far as he can, all the emperor's garments, [sic: *αἰσθητὰς*] so that one who sees the image containing the whole imperial likeness may think that he is almost seeing the emperor himself, the archetype itself. ⟦q28.6|14⟧ For the image is glorious and bright. ⟦q28.6|15⟧ So let us too not dishonour our archetype: we have been made according to the image of God; [bid:c1828b]but let us make our own image pure and precious, and worthy of the archetype. ⟦q28.6|16⟧ For if one who dishonours the image of an emperor who can be seen and is of the same kind as himself is punished, what ought we to suffer, who despise the divine image within us and do not render the image pure, as the saint said?

⟦q28.7|1⟧ 5. ⟦q28.7|2⟧ So let us honour the archetype; let us know the power of the mystery, and for what Christ died. ⟦q28.7|3+4⟧ The power of the mystery of the death of Christ is this: since we had blotted out what in us is according to the image through sin, and so had been put to death, [bid:c1828c]as the Apostle says, by trespasses and sins, God, who made us according to his own image, having pity on his own creature and his own image, became man for our sakes and accepted death on behalf of all, so that he might bring us, who had been put to death, back up to the life from which we had fallen through disobedience. ⟦q28.7|5⟧ For he himself went up on his holy cross and crucified sin, the sin for which we were also cast out of paradise, and *led captivity captive*, [n: Psal. LXVII, 19; Ephes. iv, 8.] as it is written. ⟦q28.7|6⟧ What is *led captivity captive*? ⟦q28.7|7⟧ That from the transgression of Adam the enemy had taken us captive and held us under his hand. ⟦q28.7|8⟧ So then the souls of men, going out of the body, went off to Hades; [bid:c1828d]for paradise had been shut. ⟦q28.7|9⟧ So Christ, going up to the height of the holy and life-giving cross, delivered us by his own blood from the captivity into which the enemy had taken us captive through the transgression; that is, he snatched us back out of the hand of the enemy, and, as it were, took us captive in his turn, having conquered and overthrown the one who had taken us captive. ⟦q28.7|10⟧ That is why it is said, *to take captivity captive*. ⟦q28.7|10⟧ This is the power of the mystery; for this Christ died for us, that he might bring us, who had been put to death, back up to life, as the saint said. ⟦q28.7|11⟧ So we have been delivered from Hades through the love of Christ for mankind, and it lies with us to go into paradise. ⟦q28.7|12⟧ For he no longer tyrannizes as before, nor holds us enslaved.

⟦q29.1|1⟧ 6. ⟦q29.1|2⟧ Only let us take care, brethren, and guard [1829] [bid:c1829a]ourselves from sin in act. ⟦q29.1|3+4⟧ For I have told you often before that every sin in act puts us under his hand again, since of our own will we cast ourselves down and enslave ourselves. [sic: *καταδηλοῦμεν*] For is it not a shame and great wretchedness, after he has redeemed us from Hades by his own blood, and after we hear these things, if we take ourselves off into Hades again? ⟦q29.1|5⟧ Are we not worthy of a still worse and more pitiable punishment? ⟦q29.1|6⟧ May God, who loves mankind, have mercy on us, and give us watchfulness to understand and to help ourselves, so that we may find a little mercy.

⟦q29.2|1⟧ Instruction XXIII. ⟦q29.3|1⟧ *An interpretation of the sayings of St Gregory [bid:c1829b]that are sung for the holy martyrs.*

⟦q29.4|1⟧ 1. ⟦q29.4|2⟧ It is good to sing from the words of the holy God-bearing fathers for this reason: that everywhere they are always eager to teach us all that tends to the enlightening of our souls; and in them it is set before us always to learn, from the very words fitted to the day, the power of the commemoration being kept, whether it is a feast of the Lord, or of holy martyrs, or simply of fathers, whatever holy and notable day it may be. ⟦q29.4|3⟧ So we too ought to sing with watchfulness, and to set our mind on the power of the words of the saints; so that not only the mouth, as it says in the Gerontikon, but our heart too may sing with the mouth. ⟦q29.4|4⟧ Now we learned from the former psalm a few things, according to our power, about the [bid:c1829c]holy Pascha; let us see again what St Gregory wants to teach us about the holy martyrs too. ⟦q29.4|5⟧ For the psalm about them which we said just now, from his words, says: *Living victims, rational whole burnt offerings*, and so on. ⟦q29.4|6⟧ What is *living victims*? ⟦q29.4|7⟧ A victim is anything dedicated for sacrifice to God: suppose a sheep, or an ox, or anything of the kind. ⟦q29.4|8⟧ Why then does he say of the holy martyrs, *living victims*? ⟦q29.4|9⟧ Because the sheep that is brought for sacrifice is first slaughtered and dies; and then it is divided and cut in pieces for God. ⟦q29.4|10⟧ But the holy martyrs were cut in their flesh while living, being scraped, tortured, dismembered. ⟦q29.4|11⟧ For sometimes the executioners cut off their hands too, and feet and tongues, and [bid:c1829d]gouged out their eyes, and besides this [sic: *ἐπὶ τὸ τούτων*] scraped their sides, so that the very structure and arrangement of their inward parts could be seen. ⟦q29.4|12⟧ All this, as I said, the saints endured while living, still having their souls; and that is why they are called *living victims*. ⟦q29.4|13+14⟧ And *rational whole burnt offerings*: why? ⟦q29.4|15⟧ Because a sacrifice is one thing ⟨and a whole burnt offering another⟩. ⟦q29.4|15⟧ Sometimes they do not offer the whole sheep, but only its first-fruits, as it says in the law: *The right arm, and the lobe of the liver, and the two kidneys*, [n: Levit. iii, 3, 4.] and things like these; those who offered these were bringing sacrifices; they were offering, as it were, the first-fruits; and this is [1832] [bid:c1832a]called a sacrifice. ⟦q29.4|16⟧ But it is a whole burnt offering when they burn the whole sheep or the ox, or whatever is offered, all of it, as it says there: *The head with the feet and the entrails*; [n: Levit. iv, 11.] and sometimes the hide too and the dung, and, in a word, they burn up the whole of it entirely. ⟦q29.4|17⟧ This is called a whole burning. ⟦q29.4|18⟧ In this way the sons of Israel performed both the sacrifices and the whole burnings according to the law.

⟦q29.5|1⟧ 2. ⟦q29.5|2⟧ Now those sacrifices and whole burnings were symbols of the souls that wish to be saved and to offer themselves to God. ⟦q29.5|3⟧ And I tell you a few things about these too, from what the Fathers said, so that when you read them you may lift up your understanding a little, and your soul [sic: *ἡμῶν*] may be fattened. ⟦q29.5|4⟧ The arm, they say, is the practical life; and the hands [bid:c1832b]are taken to mean this, as we have also said on various occasions. ⟦q29.5|5+6⟧ The arm is the strength of the hand. ⟦q29.5|5+6⟧ So they offered the strength of the right hand, that is, of good works; for they take the right to mean the good; and all the other things that we said, such as the lobe of the liver, and the two kidneys, and the fat from them, and the loins, and the fat on the thighs, and the heart, and the breast, and all such things. ⟦q29.5|7+8+9+10⟧ These too are likewise symbols. ⟦q29.5|7+8+9+10⟧ For all these things, as the Apostle says, happened to them as types; and they were written for our admonition. [n: I Cor. x, 11.] And I tell you how the soul, as St Gregory says, is threefold. ⟦q29.5|7+8+9+10⟧ For it has the desiring part, and the irascible part, and the rational part. ⟦q29.5|11⟧ So they offered the lobe [bid:c1832c]of the liver. ⟦q29.5|12⟧ The Fathers take the liver to mean desire; and the lobe of the liver is its tip. ⟦q29.5|13⟧ So they offered, in symbol, the tip of the desiring part, that is, its first-fruits, its finest and more precious part; which signifies loving nothing before God, preferring nothing out of all things desired to the desire for God. ⟦q29.5|14⟧ For we said that they offered him the more precious part; and the kidneys too, and the fat from them, and the loins, and the fat on the thighs, signify the same by a certain analogy; for there, they say, desire is. ⟦q29.5|15⟧ See, these are the symbols of the desiring part. ⟦q29.5|16⟧ And the symbol of the irascible part is the heart; for there, they say, anger is; and St [bid:c1832d]Basil signifies this too, saying: "Anger is a boiling of the blood around the heart." ⟦q29.5|16⟧ And the breast is the symbol of the rational part; for they take the chest to mean this. ⟦q29.5|17⟧ That is why they say that Moses, when clothing Aaron in the high-priestly vestment, [sic: *ἄνθυμα*] put the oracle on his breast, according to God's command. ⟦q29.5|18⟧ So all these things, as we said, are symbols of the soul which through the practical life, with God, cleanses itself and returns [sic: *ἐπανεχομένης*] to what is according to nature. ⟦q29.5|19⟧ For Evagrius says that a rational soul acts according to nature when its desiring part longs for virtue, its irascible part fights for it, and its rational part applies itself to the contemplation of the things that have come to be.

⟦q29.6|1+2⟧ 3. [1833] [bid:c1833a]So when they offered a sheep, or an ox, or any other such thing for sacrifices, the sons of Israel took these parts from what was offered and laid them on the altar before the Lord, and it is called a sacrifice. ⟦q29.6|3+4⟧ But it is a whole burnt offering when they offered the victim whole and entire, and burned it up just as it was, sound, complete, perfect; as we said above too, this is the symbol of the perfect; this belongs to those who say: *See, we have left everything and followed thee.* [n: Matth. xix, 27.] To this measure the Lord urged that man to come who said to him, *All these I have kept from my youth.* ⟦q29.6|5⟧ For he answered him, saying: "*One thing thou lackest.*" ⟦q29.6|6⟧ What? ⟦q29.6|7+8⟧ This: *Take up thy cross, and come, follow me.* [n: ibid. 20, 21.] So the holy martyrs in this way [bid:c1833b]offered themselves whole to God, and not only themselves, but also what was theirs and what was about them. ⟦q29.6|9⟧ For we are one thing, as St Basil says, and what is ours is another, and what is about us another; I have told you this on other occasions too. ⟦q29.6|10⟧ We are the mind and the soul; ours is the body; and what is about us is possessions and the rest of material things. ⟦q29.6|11+12⟧ So the saints offered themselves to God with all their heart, all their soul, all their strength, as it is written: *Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with all thy heart, and with all thy soul, and with all thy mind.* [n: Matth. xxii, 37; Marc. xii, 30; Luc. x, 27.] For they despised not only children and wives and glory and money and all the rest of their property, but even their own bodies; and that is why they are called whole burnt offerings. ⟦q29.6|13⟧ And rational, since man is a rational [bid:c1833c]living being, and they are perfect victims to God. ⟦q29.6|14+15⟧ Then what follows: *Sheep that know God and are known by God.* ⟦q29.6|16⟧ How *knowing God*? ⟦q29.6|17⟧ As the Lord himself showed and taught, saying: *My sheep hear my voice, and I know mine, and am known by mine.* ⟦q29.6|18⟧ Why did he say, *My sheep hear my voice*? ⟦q29.6|19⟧ Instead of saying: They obey my word and keep my commandments, and so they know me. ⟦q29.6|19⟧ For through keeping the commandments the saints draw near to God, and the more they draw near to him, the more they know him and are known by him. ⟦q29.6|20⟧ Since God knows all things, the hidden things too, and the deep things, and the things that are not, why then does he say of the saints, *known by God*? ⟦q29.6|21⟧ Because, as I said, [bid:c1833d]drawing near to him through the commandments, they know him and are known by him. ⟦q29.6|21⟧ For when someone turns away and puts himself far from another, to that extent he is said not to know ⟨him and not to be known by him; and⟩ drawing near, he is likewise said to know and to be known. ⟦q29.6|22+23+24⟧ In this sense, then, God too is said not to know sinners, in so far as they put themselves far from him. ⟦q29.6|22+23+24⟧ That is why the Lord himself says to such: *Amen I say to you, I do not know you.* [n: Matth. xxv, 12.] So the saints, as I have often said, the more they acquire the virtues through the commandments, the more they are made God's own; and the more they are made his own, the more they know him and are known by him. ⟦q29.6|22+23+24⟧ *Whose [1836] [bid:c1836a]fold is not to be trodden by wolves.* ⟦q29.6|25⟧ A fold is the name for a fenced place, where the shepherd gathers the sheep and guards them, so that they may not be torn to pieces by wolves or plundered by robbers. ⟦q29.6|26⟧ But if the fold is somewhat rotten in any part, it is found easy to get into, and is readily plotted against both by the wolves and by the robbers. ⟦q29.6|27⟧ The fold of the saints, then, is made safe and guarded on every side, as the Lord said, where thieves do not break through, [n: Matth. vi, 20.] nor can any other harmful thing plot against it. ⟦q29.6|28+29⟧ So let us pray, brethren, that we too may be counted worthy to be shepherded together with them, and to be found also in the place of that blessed delight and of their rest. ⟦q29.6|28+29⟧ For even if we have not reached the state of the saints, even if we are not worthy to be in [bid:c1836b]their glory, still we can avoid falling from paradise, if we are watchful and do ourselves a little violence; as St Clement too says: even if one is not crowned, let him yet strive to be found not far from those who are crowned. ⟦q29.6|30+31⟧ For just as in the palace there are great and splendid ranks (suppose the senate, the patricians, the generals, the prefects, the silentiaries: for these are ranks of high honour), and there are also some others serving in the same palace for a few gold pieces; yet they too are said to serve the emperor, and they are inside the palace, even if they do not have the glory of the great; still, for the present they are there. ⟦q29.6|32⟧ And it happens that they too, advancing little by little, attain great ranks and splendid dignities. ⟦q29.6|33⟧ So let us too strive to escape sin in act, so that for the present we may get clear of Hades; and so, through God's love for mankind, we can attain even the very entrance into paradise, by the prayers of all the saints. ⟦q29.6|34⟧ Amen.
